Time Series: Non-ergodic AR(1) and EUR/USD

A process that is stationary but not ergodic, simulated and proved, and a forecasting exercise on EUR/USD where nothing is left to forecast.

A single long time series is supposed to tell you everything about the process behind it. This project has two cases that test that idea from opposite ends. In the first, a process is stationary on paper but each of its paths lives at its own level, so one path says nothing about the others. In the second, EUR/USD returns, there is so little structure that even careful forecasting machinery has nothing to find.

\(\theta = 0.9\)AR(1) coefficient of the simulated process; its mean is drawn once per path from \(U[-5, 5]\)
0.99 vs 0.01autocorrelation at lag 40: across paths against inside a single path
2,815daily EUR/USD log-returns, January 2015 to October 2025
ARIMA(0,0,0)the model auto.arima selects: white noise

Course project, Time Series and Financial Time Series, Sapienza University of Rome, October to December 2025. R, forecast, tseries, boot.

A process whose paths do not agree

Take \(Z_t = \theta Z_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t\) with \(\lvert\theta\rvert < 1\), where the noise is \(\varepsilon_t \sim N(Y, \sigma^2)\) and the mean \(Y\) is drawn once, uniformly on \([-5, 5]\), and then kept: it does not change with \(t\). Given \(Y = y\) this is an ordinary AR(1) that settles around \(y/(1-\theta)\). Averaging over \(y\) with the laws of total expectation and total variance, the process has mean 0, variance \(\sigma^2/(1-\theta^2) + \mathrm{Var}(Y)/(1-\theta)^2\) and autocovariance

\[\gamma(h) = \theta^{\lvert h\rvert}\,\frac{\sigma^2}{1-\theta^2} + \frac{\mathrm{Var}(Y)}{(1-\theta)^2}\]

which depends on \(h\) only, so the process is weakly stationary. It is not ergodic: the second term does not fade as \(h\) grows, because each path carries its own level. Along one long path the sample mean converges to \(y/(1-\theta)\), not to the mean of the process.

simulate_path <- function(theta, var_eps = 1, T = 300) {
  y   <- runif(1, -5, 5)                      # drawn once per path: this is what breaks ergodicity
  eps <- rnorm(T + 1, mean = y, sd = sqrt(var_eps))
  Z   <- numeric(T)
  prev <- eps[1]
  for (t in 1:T) {
    Z[t] <- theta * prev + eps[t + 1]
    prev <- Z[t]
  }
  list(Z = Z, y = y)                          # the path settles near y / (1 - theta)
}

Twelve simulated paths, each with its own draw of Y, and the average over 2,000 paths in grey. Each path settles at its own level while the average stays near zero.

The autocorrelation shows the same thing in a different way. Inside a single path it decays like \(\theta^h\), which is what the sample ACF of one long series recovers. Across paths it does not decay: the shared term \(\mathrm{Var}(Y)/(1-\theta)^2\) keeps it close to 1. The estimator computed from one series is consistent for the first and cannot reach the second, which needs many realisations.

Autocorrelation function, theory (lines) and simulation (markers): one path of 4,000 observations against 3,000 independent paths observed at one date.

I also built the simulation as an interactive Shiny app, where \(\theta\), the noise variance, the length of the series and the number of Monte Carlo paths can be changed live: open the app.

EUR/USD, where there is nothing to find

EUR/USD is one of the most liquid markets there is, and its dynamics are close to a random walk, which makes it a hard test for any forecasting model. I took 2,815 daily log-returns from January 2015 to October 2025. The ADF and Phillips–Perron tests reject a unit root (both at the 1% limit of the tables) and KPSS does not reject stationarity (p of 0.10 or more). The first autocorrelation is -0.007.

Five ARIMA models, compared by information criteria:

model log-likelihood AIC BIC
ARIMA(0,0,0) 10,917.6 −21,833.3 −21,827.3
ARIMA(1,0,0) 10,917.7 −21,829.5 −21,811.6
ARIMA(0,0,1) 10,917.7 −21,829.5 −21,811.6
ARIMA(1,0,1) 10,917.7 −21,827.5 −21,803.7
ARIMA(6,0,0) 10,921.9 −21,827.8 −21,780.3

auto.arima selects ARIMA(0,0,0) with zero mean, a Gaussian white noise. An AR(1) or an MA(1) adds 0.1 to the log-likelihood, six lags of AR add 4.3: not enough to pay for the parameters, and none of the AR coefficients is larger than its standard error. The residuals show no remaining structure.

eurusd_ret <- na.omit(diff(log(Cl(`EURUSD=X`))))          # daily log-returns
eurusd_ts  <- ts(as.numeric(eurusd_ret), frequency = 252)

auto_fit <- auto.arima(eurusd_ts)                         # picks ARIMA(0,0,0): white noise
fc_normal <- forecast(auto_fit, h = 30)                   # Gaussian intervals
fc_boot   <- forecast(auto_fit, h = 30,                   # resample the residuals instead
                      bootstrap = TRUE, npaths = 1000)

With a white-noise model the forecast is the mean, zero, at every horizon, and the interval keeps the same width: ±0.98% for the next day, and for the next 30. I compared three ways of building that interval: the Gaussian one, a bootstrap that resamples the residuals, and a moving block bootstrap with blocks of 14 days that keeps local dependence.

The last 120 daily log-returns and the 95% intervals for the next 30 trading days from the three methods; the forecast itself is zero.

The three nearly coincide. With a model that has no dynamics that is close to inevitable: there is nothing for the resampling to change except the shape of the residuals (residual bootstrap) and the uncertainty about their variance (block bootstrap), and neither moves much. Bagging gives the same message. I averaged the forecasts of 100 ARIMA models fitted on block-bootstrapped copies of the training data and compared them with a single ARIMA on a 30-day holdout: RMSE of 0.00336 for the single model and 0.00341 for the bagged one, so the extra machinery does not help.

That is the result I would expect from daily exchange-rate returns, and it echoes my bachelor’s thesis on why exchange rates are so hard to forecast: when the series behaves like white noise, a more elaborate method cannot recover predictability that is not in the data.
